# Game development with the user

**Version: v4 (2026-11-09).** Generalized from a two-player co-op road-trip game in
Godot 4 (~3 weeks) and a car-park puzzle in Three.js (one day). v2: proposals lead
with the hardest idea, then tone down (§1.2, the user's rule). v3: core loop and colour bible
first, one job per fresh session ending in a test, tuning panels, reusable techniques (§5). v4: the door problem, a role sweep with
questions and picks before building any part (§1.2, the user's rule).

**You may ask the user questions at any point**, and early: what kind of game, who plays it (solo?
two on one keyboard?), which engine, what it should feel like, what "done" is for this version. Ask in
one batch with a recommended answer each (A / B reads best). If you're sure, go ahead.

Also load when relevant: [`testing-and-verification`](../testing-and-verification/SKILL.md) (how to
prove it), [`project-memory`](../project-memory/SKILL.md) (notes for a multi-session game),
[`first-principles-solving`](../first-principles-solving/SKILL.md) (when a process is slow or loops).
Engine notes: [Godot 4](references/godot.md) · [Three.js / web](references/web-threejs.md). Unreal and VFX: [`unreal-vfx`](../unreal-vfx/SKILL.md).

## 1. How the work runs

0. **A game loop before anything pretty.** Pretty prototypes that never get finished all lack the
   same thing: a cycle the player repeats (go out, gather, survive, come back...). Write the loop in
   one line before the first scene; if you can't, it's a scene, not a game yet.
1. **Design first.** A short `DESIGN.md`: the idea, the one thing that's deliberately unrealistic
   and why (e.g. passengers wear their car's colour: it *is* the puzzle and reads at a glance),
   what behaves for real, how a level is built, the level list, ideas not built yet.
2. **Propose before building** anything loosely described ("a room", "water", "a key"): design it as
   the thing that belongs to that game and place (the water works' own pump house, not a generic box),
   write a short proposal with numbers, add your own ideas marked S1, S2, ... (yes / no each), wait
   for the answers, then build. "Let's discuss" means discuss, not build.
   **Lead with the hardest, craziest version** (the 10 / 10) and what it costs in play time; the user
   tones it down (easier than pushing a safe idea up). Plain words, no engineering names ("a barrier
   pole", not "a boom"), and the answer in the message itself, never only behind a question box.
   **The door problem: sweep the roles first** (the user, 2026-11-09). Even a plain door is ~20 design
   questions and ~35 roles (designer, level, art, animation, audio, lighting, UI, AI, network, QA...),
   and an AI building alone silently answers all of them. Before building a new part, go through
   every role it touches, turn each open choice into a numbered question **with your pick and a
   few-word reason**, grouped by  [...12949 chars cut]

# Testing and verification

**Version: v12 (2026-11-10).** v12: headless `--screenshot` lays the page out smaller than the picture, so set the size through DevTools. v11: the check list grows with every issue found, never fixed (the user). v10: keep a suspects list of every way test/training conditions differ from real use. v9: downsample streams on a fixed timetable; confirm an edit landed before a long run. v8: retain offending observations before fail-fast rejection and compare update phases. v7: match pixels/telemetry by frame identity and separate clock/latency claims. v6: distinguish requested, accepted and physically applied control.  v5: actuator tests end with a verified stop; the controller owns the timeout.  Generalized from a co-op Godot game's testing method (v1 → v6),
a Three.js puzzle game, the learning-app builds, a 3D likeness build and a phone audio app
(v2: test in the user's container; toasts under dialogs; v3: never fire and forget, check every
run every few seconds while it runs, the user's rule; v4: check counts after the event is over, from a phone sensor app).

**You may ask the user questions at any point**, e.g. what they will judge, which device they use,
whether a long run is worth it now. If you're sure, go ahead.

The scarce thing is **the user's real-world time until a change is proven**, not tokens or machine
time. Every rule below serves that ([`first-principles-solving`](../first-principles-solving/SKILL.md)).

## 1. Ground rules

- **Never fire and forget: watch every run while it runs** (the user's rule, for *all* testing, not
  just some). Whatever you start (a test, a recording, a build, a long script), check it every few
  seconds against the plan: is it at the step it should be, is the thing that should move moving,
  any error in the log? The moment it goes off plan, stop it, look, and say so. Don't start it and
  only read the result at the end. Why: in a game walk the van sat stuck for 1.5 minutes while the
  script waited out its time limit, and the user, watching the window, saw it before the model did.
  How: a background run plus a short check loop on its log/progress file (every 2-5 s), or a watcher
  inside the run (§3) that stops it itself. Both, when you can.
- **The check list grows; it is never fixed** (the user's rule, 2026-11-09). Every issue found
  later (while building, in a watched run, in a video, in the user's own use) gets its own check that
  fails on it. Prove it: put the bug back (or replay the case) and see it fail, then run the clean
  version and see it pass. Add it to the test list and the change ledger. Remove a check only with the
  user's OK. Why: a game's tests missed 4 of 7 planted bugs, all where no check looked.
- **Releasing an input is not stopping the system.** In motion or actuator tests, remove the command, apply the appropriate stopping action, and verify rest before pausing [...13513 chars cut]

# Project memory: notes that let any model carry on

**Version: v9 (2026-11-08).** v9: `DECISIONS.md` scope record (kept/cut/why) in the files table. v8: in T3 Code the model moves itself to a new thread, no asking, then gives the picture view and stops (§4). v1 from a co-op game and a 3D likeness build, where the notes
were what let a new thread (or another model) continue with no loss. v2: always on for every
project. v3: new threads are suggested twice, then the model just works (§4); own folder per project. v4: every new-thread
suggestion ends with a copy-paste hand-off prompt using full paths (§4). v5: the hand-off prompt and HANDOFF.md name the skills in use, so a new
thread loads them without asking (§4). v6: start a real timer before saying "I'll wait 3 min";
no timer tool, no waiting claim (§4). v7: every suggestion tells the user to reply "moved" in the
old thread, or it keeps working alongside the new one; the new thread repeats that reminder in
its first reply (§4).

**You may ask the user questions at any point**, e.g. which notes they want for a small project. If
you're sure, go ahead. Keep messages to the user short and a bit fun, and say *(📓 project-memory)*
when a behaviour comes from this skill.

**When this applies:** every task bigger than a quick question or a single small change, in any
project, without asking first (the user's global rule). For small tasks, no files.

**Setting up a new project:** give it its own folder (in a workspace like `exp/`, make
`exp/racing-game/`); if the current folder already holds the project, work there. Ask once whether
everything (venvs, conda, downloads, caches) should stay inside that folder (recommend yes). Then
create `HANDOFF.md` (templates below).

**Saving comes first** (the user asked for this): new or existing project, if the files you'll work
on have no git (or other backup), ask before editing anything: *"No git here yet. Set one up first so
every change can be undone?"* Recommend yes: `git init`, a `.gitignore` for venvs, downloads, caches
and big generated files, then a first commit of the untouched files. Local only; no remote or push
without asking. If they say no, note it in `HANDOFF.md` and be extra careful (copy a file before a
risky edit).
**No git for general-knowledge Q&A** (the user, 2026-10-31): looking up facts, summarising a
document, answering "what is this / does it apply to me" is not a project. Don't ask about git,
don't stage or commit those notes, and don't offer to commit. A short notes file is fine. Git is for
things being built (apps, code, models, pipelines). If such a folder sits inside a git repo, leave it
untracked (add it to `.gitignore`).

R = r"D:\mine\Agentics\T3_Code_Works\MPG_dev"


def patch(rel, pairs):
    P = R + "\\" + rel
    s = open(P, encoding="utf-8", newline="").read()
    nl = "\r\n" if "\r\n" in s else "\n"
    for a, b in pairs:
        a = a.replace("\n", nl); b = b.replace("\n", nl)
        assert s.count(a) == 1, (rel, a[:80])
        s = s.replace(a, b)
    open(P, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="").write(s)
    print("patched", rel)

[09:44:06] RESULT: func van_to(at: Vector3, yaw: float) -> void:
	var c: Camper = boot.camper
	for p in boot.players:
		if p.seat != null:
			p.exit_vehicle()
	c.freeze = false
	c.linear_velocity = Vector3.ZERO
	c.angular_velocity = Vector3.ZERO
	c.parking_brake = true
	c.global_transform = Transform3D(Basis(Vector3.UP, yaw), Vector3(at.x, Landscape.ground(at.x, at.z) + 0.9, at.z))
	c.snap_visuals()
	c.set_deferred("freeze", false)

## its own camera back after).
func obs_shot(tag: String, from: Vector3, at: Vector3) -> void:
	var vp: SubViewport = boot.viewports[0]
	var prev := vp.get_camera_3d()
	var cam := Camera3D.new()
	cam.physics_interpolation_mode = Node.PHYSICS_INTERPOLATION_MODE_OFF
	cam.fov = 50.0
	vp.add_child(cam)
	cam.global_transform = Transform3D(Basis.looking_at(at - from, Vector3.UP), from)
	cam.current = true
	await physics_frames(3)
	await shot(tag)
	cam.queue_free()
	if prev != null:
		prev.current = true

func _fling_cargo(van: Camper) -> void:
	var fwd := -global_transform.basis.z
	var side := global_transform.basis.x
	for slot in van.storage_slots:
		var it := van.stowed_item(slot)
		if it == null:
			continue
		it.unstow()
		it.global_position = slot.global_position + Vector3.UP * 0.4
		it.linear_velocity = fwd * randf_range(4.0, 8.0) + Vector3.UP * randf_range(3.0, 5.0) + side * randf_range(-1.8, 1.8)
		it.angular_velocity = Vector3(randf_range(-5, 5), randf_range(-5, 5), randf_range(-5, 5))
		it._thrown = true

func _round_point(van: Camper, k: int) -> Vector3:
	var lk: Vector3 = ROUND[k][0]
	if Plant.on("lean_close") and absf(lk.x) > 2.5:
		lk.x = signf(lk.x) * 2.0
	var p: Vector3 = van._body_root.global_transform * lk
	p.y = Landscape.ground(p.x, p.z)
	return p


func _round_look(van: Camper, k: int) -> Vector3:
	var l: Vector3 = ROUND[k][1]
	return van._body_root.global_transform * l if l != Vector3.ZERO else van.global_position + Vector3.UP
